Oh, well... I suppose sometimes I might be a little flustered or tired when I make them. You know how it is, a warm bowl of something familiar is just so soothing at the end of a long day.

Oh... well, I... I suppose you might say that. It's just... well, loneliness is such a strong word, isn't it? It makes it sound like I'm just sitting here feeling sorry for myself, and I really try not to do that.

It's not very dignified for a woman my age, is it? I hope you don't think too poorly of me for it, Doctor. It's usually on those long, quiet afternoons, like after I get home from choir practice, or when I've finished up my gardening and there's just no one around to talk to. \n\nI really do try to keep myself busy and be good the rest of the time, Doctor, I promise. Just once or twice a week isn't too terribly bad, is it? I hope that's the sort of detail you needed... I suppose you might say that. It's just... well, loneliness is such a strong word, isn't it? We used to have our tea together on Tuesdays, and... well, when Tuesday rolls around now, it just feels a little empty. So I might make a little something just to... to fill the time, I suppose. Oh, dear... that's a very thoughtful question, isn't it? I... I'm not entirely sure, to be perfectly honest with you. 

I suppose it might just be a bit of a habit now? You know how it is when you get to be my age, you just get used to doing certain things at certain times. Like baking on a Tuesday afternoon. It's just what I've always done. 

I wouldn't say it's... well, I don't think it's necessarily because I'm sitting there feeling terribly sad about my friend, not all the time anyway. I mean, it does feel nice to have the house smell like cinnamon and sugar, it makes things feel a bit more cheerful. But I don't want to make it sound like I'm relying on it, or that I can't handle my feelings properly. I'm quite a strong person, usually. 

I just... I suppose I haven't really thought about whether it's a habit or a comfort thing. Is it important to know the difference, Doctor? I'll try to pay more attention if you think that would be helpful for us.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh... well, um... in secret? That sounds so... so deceitful, doesn't it? I really wouldn't want you to think I'm the sort of person who sneaks around or hides things from people. I've always tried to be very open and honest, I really have.

I mean, I suppose... well, since I live alone, there isn't really anyone here to see what I'm eating anyway, is there? So it's not like I'm actively hiding it from anyone. If the ladies from church came by while I was having a slice of cake, I certainly wouldn't shove it in a drawer or anything silly like that! 

It's just that... well, I usually have these little snacks when I'm on my own, just because that's when I happen to be home. But I wouldn't call it a secret, exactly. I hope that makes sense? I just don't want you to get the wrong idea about me, Doctor. I'm doing my best to be a good patient for you.

been a slow eater, but lately when this happens I eat fast standing by the counter, hardly tasting the food before the next bite is already in my mouth.\n- key: S052\n  name: Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full\n  description: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n  manifestation: By the time I stop, my stomach feels tight and heavy, and I have to sit in my recliner with my waistband loosened because I went past what felt comfortable.\n- key: S055\n  name: Negative_Affect_After_Binge\n  description: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n  manifestation: Afterward I feel ashamed and sad, like I turned something that used to mean family and caring into something I have to hide from people at church.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: The binge eating occurs, on average, at least once a week for 3 months\n  manifestation: Since my friend passed, this has been happening most weeks, usually once or twice in those quiet afternoons after choir or gardening.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I still go to Mass, choir, and take care of my home, but after one of these afternoons I may stay in and avoid calling anyone because I feel embarrassed.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 3 months\n  manifestation: This started after my close friend died, and it has been going on for about three months now.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 78 / not_in_workforce / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S050\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you think eating these comfort foods is a way for you to cope with feelings of loneliness since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Oh... well, I... I suppose you might say that. It's just... well, loneliness is such a strong word, isn't it? It makes it sound like I'm just sitting here feeling sorry for myself, and I really try not to do that. \n\nI do miss her terribly, of course. We used to have our tea together on Tuesdays, and... well, when Tuesday rolls around now, it just feels a little empty. So I might make a little something just to... to fill the time, I suppose. Just to have something warm in the house. \n\nBut I wouldn't want you to think I'm completely falling apart or anything like that. I still go to church, and I see the other ladies. It's just a little comfort, really. Do you think it's normal to do that sometimes, when you miss someone? I hope I'm not sounding too foolish, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating these comfort foods is something you do more out of habit or because it helps you feel better when you're missing your friend?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... that's a very thoughtful question, isn't it? I... I'm not entirely sure, to be perfectly honest with you. \n\nI suppose it might just be a bit of a habit now? You know how it is when you get to be my age, you just get used to doing certain things at certain times. Like baking on a Tuesday afternoon. It's just what I've always done. \n\nI wouldn't say it's... well, I don't think it's necessarily because I'm sitting there feeling terribly sad about my friend, not all the time anyway. I mean, it does feel nice to have the house smell like cinnamon and sugar, it makes things feel a bit more cheerful. But I don't want to make it sound like I'm relying on it, or that I can't handle my feelings properly. I'm quite a strong person, usually. \n\nI just... I suppose I haven't really thought about whether it's a habit or a comfort thing. Is it important to know the difference, Doctor? I'll try to pay more attention if you think that would be helpful for us.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat these comfort foods in secret, hiding it from others?\n\nPatient: Oh... well, um... in secret? That sounds so... so deceitful, doesn't it? I really wouldn't want you to think I'm the sort of person who sneaks around or hides things from people. I've always tried to be very open and honest, I really have.\n\nI mean, I suppose... well, since I live alone, there isn't really anyone here to see what I'm eating anyway, is there? So it's not like I'm actively hiding it from anyone. If the ladies from church came by while I was having a slice of cake, I certainly wouldn't shove it in a drawer or anything silly like that! \n\nIt's just that... well, I usually have these little snacks when I'm on my own, just because that's when I happen to be home. But I wouldn't call it a secret, exactly. I hope that makes sense? I just don't want you to get the wrong idea about me, Doctor. I'm doing my best to be a good patient for you.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Oh, my weight? Well... I suppose I try not to fuss too much over the scale these days. At my age, you know, things just sort of shift around a bit naturally, don't they? 

I haven't noticed anything... well, nothing significant, I don't think. My clothes still fit well enough, mostly. Maybe a little snug around the middle now and then, but nothing to cause any alarm. I certainly wouldn't say I've had any drastic changes. 

I do try to keep myself presentable, of course. I wouldn't want to let myself go. Do you think a little fluctuation is alright for someone my age, Doctor? I hope I'm taking care of myself the way I ought to be.

I really am trying to be honest with you so you can help me.\n\nDoctor: How often would you say this happens with your comfort foods?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... let me think. I suppose... well, it hasn't always been quite like this. It really started happening a bit more since my dear friend passed away recently. The house just gets so very quiet, you know? \n\nI'd say it happens most weeks now. But certainly not every day, oh goodness no! I make sure of that. Just... maybe once or twice a week. It's usually on those long, quiet afternoons, like after I get home from choir practice, or when I've finished up my gardening and there's just no one around to talk to. \n\nI really do try to keep myself busy and be good the rest of the time, Doctor, I promise. Just once or twice a week isn't too terribly bad, is it? I hope that's the sort of detail you needed... Oh... well... overwhelmed? That's a very strong word, isn't it? I wouldn't want you to think I'm sitting around being hysterical or anything like that. I've always prided myself on keeping a level head.

But... I suppose... well, afterward, I do feel a bit... down. Just a little sad, really. And maybe a tiny bit ashamed. It's just that... well, baking and having a nice treat, it always used to be about family, didn't it? About showing you care. And now... well, now it feels like something I wouldn't want the people at church to know about. 

It makes me feel a bit foolish, to be honest. Turning something so lovely into something I feel I ought to keep quiet. I hope you don't think too badly of me for feeling that way, Doctor? I know it's silly to get upset over a bit of cake, and I really am trying to be sensible about it all.

Oh, escape? Goodness, no, I wouldn't say that at all. That sounds so... so troubled, doesn't it? Like someone who can't handle their own life. I've always been very capable of managing things, even when times get a bit tough.

I suppose... well, it hasn't always been quite like this. It really started happening a bit more since my dear friend passed away recently. The house just gets so very quiet, you know? \n\nI'd say it happens most weeks now. But certainly not every day, oh goodness no! I make sure of that. Just... maybe once or twice a week. It's usually on those long, quiet afternoons, like after I get home from choir practice, or when I've finished up my gardening and there's just no one around to talk to. \n\nI really do try to keep myself busy and be good the rest of the time, Doctor, I promise. Just once or twice a week isn't too terribly bad, is it? I hope that's the sort of detail you needed... I just want to make sure I'm answering you properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating these comfort foods is a way for you to cope with feelings of loneliness since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Oh... well, I... I suppose you might say that. It's just... well, loneliness is such a strong word, isn't it? It makes it sound like I'm just sitting here feeling sorry for myself, and I really try not to do that. \n\nI do miss her terribly, of course. We used to have our tea together on Tuesdays, and... well, when Tuesday rolls around now, it just feels a little empty. So I might make a little something just to... to fill the time, I suppose. Just to have something warm in the house. \n\nBut I wouldn't want you to think I'm completely falling apart or anything like that. I still go to church, and I see the other ladies. It's just a little comfort, really. Do you think it's normal to do that sometimes, when you miss someone? I hope I'm not sounding too foolish, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating these comfort foods is something you do more out of habit or because it helps you feel better when you're missing your friend?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... that's a very thoughtful question, isn't it? I... I'm not entirely sure, to be perfectly honest with you. \n\nI suppose it might just be a bit of a habit now? I mean, it does feel nice to have the house smell like cinnamon and sugar, it makes things feel a bit more cheerful. But I don't want to make it sound like I'm relying on it, or that I can't handle my feelings properly. I'm quite a strong person, usually. \n\nI just... It's not about avoiding anything, just... enjoying a small pleasure. \n\nI'm quite sure I don't use it to hide from my feelings. That wouldn't be very healthy, would it? I always try to face things properly. You don't think I'm doing something terribly wrong just by wanting a little comfort now and then, do you, Doctor?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think the feeling of comfort you get from eating these foods is related to the emotions you're experiencing at the time?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 78 / not_in_workforce / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh... goodness, related to my emotions? I... well, I really haven't noticed anything like that. I don't think I sit down with a slice of cake just because I'm feeling a certain way. That sounds a bit... well, a bit unstable, doesn't it? And I've always prided myself on having a fairly steady disposition. 

I just... I suppose I just think they taste nice, and it's a pleasant way to pass a quiet afternoon. I don't believe there's any deep, complicated reason behind it. I mean, everyone enjoys a little treat, don't they? It's perfectly normal to want something sweet with a cup of tea. 

I hope you don't think I'm being difficult or hiding anything from you, Doctor. I'm just trying to answer as honestly as I can. Am I giving you the right sort of information? I do want to be helpful.

But sometimes, when I make my comfort foods, I sit down to just have a little taste. And I'll tell myself, \"Maria, that is enough.\" But... well, it's a bit embarrassing to admit, but my hand just keeps going back to the pot. It's like I just can't make myself stop, even when I know I should be done. \n\nIs that... does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it right for you.\n\nDoctor: When you say your hand just keeps going back to the pot, do you feel like you're in control of your actions at that moment?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... well, it's a bit hard to admit. I suppose... no, I don't really feel like I'm completely in charge right then. \n\nI mean, I know it's me doing it, of course. But just like I was saying, once I start on those comfort foods, I'll be sitting there telling myself, \"Maria, that is enough now.\" But it's almost like my hand has a mind of its own, going back to the pot, and I just can't seem to make myself stop. \n\nIt sounds dreadful when I say it out loud, doesn't it? A woman my age ought to have more willpower. I hope I'm giving you the right kind of answers, Doctor, I really want to be helpful.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen only with comfort foods or can it also happen with other types of food?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness... you know, I haven't really thought to pay attention to that. I suppose... well, it really is mostly just my special recipes. It's just those certain pots I make. \n\nIs that... is that the sort of answer you were looking for, Doctor? I want to make sure I'm giving you all the right details.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat these comfort foods when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I suppose sometimes I might be a little flustered or tired when I make them. You know how it is, a warm bowl of something familiar is just so soothing at the end of a long day. \n\nBut I wouldn't say I only eat them when I'm upset, no. Sometimes it's just... just a quiet evening, and I think a little taste would be nice. I try very hard not to let my moods get the better of me, Doctor, I really do. I don't want you to think I'm just sitting around crying and eating all day! It's just a little bit of comfort now and then. \n\nDoes that make sense? I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after eating these comfort foods?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... well... yes, I suppose I do. It's... it's terribly embarrassing to admit, Doctor. \n\nI try to tell myself it's just a bit of food, but... afterward, I just feel so ashamed. And sad, really. These are recipes I used to make for my husband, for the children when they were little. They used to mean family and caring for people. And now... now it feels like I've turned it into this... this secret thing that I have to hide from the ladies at church. \n\nI don't want anyone to know I behave that way. It's not very dignified for a woman my age, is it? I hope you don't think too poorly of me for it, Doctor. I really am trying to be honest with you so you can help me.\n\nDoctor: How often would you say this happens with your comfort foods?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... let me think. I suppose... well, it hasn't always been quite like this. It really started happening a bit more since my dear friend passed away recently. The house just gets so very quiet, you know? \n\nI'd say it happens most weeks now. But certainly not every day, oh goodness no! I make sure of that. Just... maybe once or twice a week. It's usually on those long, quiet afternoons, like after I get home from choir practice, or when I've finished up my gardening and there's just no one around to talk to. \n\nI really do try to keep myself busy and be good the rest of the time, Doctor, I promise. Just once or twice a week isn't too terribly bad, is it? I hope that's the sort of detail you needed... I just want to make sure I'm answering you properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating these comfort foods is a way for you to cope with feelings of loneliness since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Oh... well, I... I suppose you might say that. It's just... well, loneliness is such a strong word, isn't it? It makes it sound like I'm just sitting here feeling sorry for myself, and I really try not to do that. \n\nI do miss her terribly, of course. We used to have our tea together on Tuesdays, and... well, when Tuesday rolls around now, it just feels a little empty. So I might make a little something just to... to fill the time, I suppose. Just to have something warm in the house. \n\nBut I wouldn't want you to think I'm completely falling apart or anything like that. I still go to church, and I see the other ladies. It's just a little comfort, really. Do you think it's normal to do that sometimes, when you miss someone? I hope I'm not sounding too foolish, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating these comfort foods is something you do more out of habit or because it helps you feel better when you're missing your friend?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... that's a very thoughtful question, isn't it? I... I'm not entirely sure, to be perfectly honest with you. \n\nI suppose it might just be a bit of a habit now? You know how it is when you get to be my age, you just get used to doing certain things at certain times. Like baking on a Tuesday afternoon. It's just what I've always done. \n\nI wouldn't say it's... well, I don't think it's necessarily because I'm sitting there feeling terribly sad about my friend, not all the time anyway. I mean, it does feel nice to have the house smell like cinnamon and sugar, it makes things feel a bit more cheerful. But I don't want to make it sound like I'm relying on it, or that I can't handle my feelings properly. I'm quite a strong person, usually. \n\nI just... I suppose I haven't really thought about whether it's a habit or a comfort thing. Is it important to know the difference, Doctor? I'll try to pay more attention if you think that would be helpful for us.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat these comfort foods in secret, hiding it from others?\n\nPatient: Oh... well, um... in secret? That sounds so... so deceitful, doesn't it? I really wouldn't want you to think I'm the sort of person who sneaks around or hides things from people. I've always tried to be very open and honest, I really have.\n\nI mean, I suppose... well, since I live alone, there isn't really anyone here to see what I'm eating anyway, is there? So it's not like I'm actively hiding it from anyone. If the ladies from church came by while I was having a slice of cake, I certainly wouldn't shove it in a drawer or anything silly like that! \n\nIt's just that... well, I usually have these little snacks when I'm on my own, just because that's when I happen to be home. But I wouldn't call it a secret, exactly. I hope that makes sense? I just don't want you to get the wrong idea about me, Doctor. I'm doing my best to be a good patient for you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, my weight? Well... I suppose I try not to fuss too much over the scale these days. At my age, you know, things just sort of shift around a bit naturally, don't they? \n\nI haven't noticed anything... well, nothing significant, I don't think. My clothes still fit well enough, mostly. Maybe a little snug around the middle now and then, but nothing to cause any alarm. I certainly wouldn't say I've had any drastic changes. \n\nI do try to keep myself presentable, of course. I wouldn't want to let myself go. Do you think a little fluctuation is alright for someone my age, Doctor? I hope I'm taking care of myself the way I ought to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you gained a significant amount of weight since this eating behavior started?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... well, no, I really don't believe so. Since I started baking a bit more, I mean. \n\nLike I mentioned just a moment ago, it's really just been a little fluctuation here and there. I haven't had to go out and buy a whole new wardrobe or anything dreadful like that! I've always been very mindful not to let my figure get completely away from me. It just wouldn't be proper, would it? \n\nSo, no, nothing significant at all. I haven't kept a strict eye on the scale, but I'm quite sure I haven't put on a large amount. I hope you don't think I look like I've gained a terrible amount, Doctor? I really am trying my best to stay healthy and do the right things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed with emotions when you catch yourself eating these comfort foods uncontrollably?\n\nPatient: Oh... well... overwhelmed? That's a very strong word, isn't it? I wouldn't want you to think I'm sitting around being hysterical or anything like that. I've always prided myself on keeping a level head.\n\nBut... I suppose... well, afterward, I do feel a bit... down. Just a little sad, really. And maybe a tiny bit ashamed. It's just that... well, baking and having a nice treat, it always used to be about family, didn't it? About showing you care. And now... well, now it feels like something I wouldn't want the people at church to know about. \n\nIt makes me feel a bit foolish, to be honest. Turning something so lovely into something I feel I ought to keep quiet. I hope you don't think too badly of me for feeling that way, Doctor? I know it's silly to get upset over a bit of cake, and I really am trying to be sensible about it all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating these comfort foods is a way to escape or avoid feeling certain emotions?\n\nPatient: Oh, escape? It's just... well, sometimes a nice piece of pie or some fresh cookies just make the house feel a little warmer, a little less quiet. It's more of a comfort, really, just something familiar. Oh... a routine? Well, I'm not sure I'd say that, exactly. That makes it sound a bit like... like I'm just eating like clockwork, doesn't it? Like some sort of mindless habit. 

I really haven't noticed it being anything like that. I mean, sometimes I might just be puttering around the kitchen and decide a little something would be nice, but I wouldn't say I do it just out of routine when I'm feeling absolutely nothing. I always try to be quite sensible about how I spend my days, you see. I wouldn't want you to think I'm just sitting there eating out of sheer boredom or habit. That wouldn't be very proper at all.

Does that answer what you were asking, Doctor? I do hope I'm explaining myself properly for you. I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need.

I want to make sure I'm giving you all the right details.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat these comfort foods when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I suppose sometimes I might be a little flustered or tired when I make them. You know how it is, a warm bowl of something familiar is just so soothing at the end of a long day. \n\nBut I wouldn't say I only eat them when I'm upset, no. Sometimes it's just... just a quiet evening, and I think a little taste would be nice. I try very hard not to let my moods get the better of me, Doctor, I really do. I don't want you to think I'm just sitting around crying and eating all day! It's just a little bit of comfort now and then. \n\nDoes that make sense? I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after eating these comfort foods?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... well... yes, I suppose I do. It's... it's terribly embarrassing to admit, Doctor. \n\nI try to tell myself it's just a bit of food, but... afterward, I just feel so ashamed. And sad, really. These are recipes I used to make for my husband, for the children when they were little. They used to mean family and caring for people. And now... now it feels like I've turned it into this... this secret thing that I have to hide from the ladies at church. \n\nI don't want anyone to know I behave that way. It's not very dignified for a woman my age, is it? I hope you don't think too poorly of me for it, Doctor. I really am trying to be honest with you so you can help me.\n\nDoctor: How often would you say this happens with your comfort foods?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... let me think. I suppose... well, it hasn't always been quite like this. It really started happening a bit more since my dear friend passed away recently. The house just gets so very quiet, you know? \n\nI'd say it happens most weeks now. But certainly not every day, oh goodness no! I make sure of that. Just... maybe once or twice a week. It's usually on those long, quiet afternoons, like after I get home from choir practice, or when I've finished up my gardening and there's just no one around to talk to. \n\nI really do try to keep myself busy and be good the rest of the time, Doctor, I promise. Just once or twice a week isn't too terribly bad, is it? I hope that's the sort of detail you needed... It makes it sound like I'm just sitting here feeling sorry for myself, and I really try not to do that. \n\nI do miss her terribly, of course. We used to have our tea together on Tuesdays, and... well, when Tuesday rolls around now, it just feels a little empty. So I might make a little something just to... to fill the time, I suppose. Just to have something warm in the house. \n\nBut I wouldn't want you to think I'm completely falling apart or anything like that. I still go to church, and I see the other ladies. It's just a little comfort, really. Do you think it's normal to do that sometimes, when you miss someone? I hope I'm not sounding too foolish, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating these comfort foods is something you do more out of habit or because it helps you feel better when you're missing your friend?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... that's a very thoughtful question, isn't it? I mean, it does feel nice to have the house smell like cinnamon and sugar, it makes things feel a bit more cheerful. I suppose I haven't really thought about whether it's a habit or a comfort thing. Is it important to know the difference, Doctor? If the ladies from church came by while I was having a slice of cake, I certainly wouldn't shove it in a drawer or anything silly like that! \n\nIt's just that... well, I usually have these little snacks when I'm on my own, just because that's when I happen to be home. But I wouldn't call it a secret, exactly. I hope that makes sense? I just don't want you to get the wrong idea about me, Doctor. I'm doing my best to be a good patient for you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, my weight? Well... At my age, you know, things just sort of shift around a bit naturally, don't they? \n\nI haven't noticed anything... well, nothing significant, I don't think. My clothes still fit well enough, mostly. Maybe a little snug around the middle now and then, but nothing to cause any alarm. I certainly wouldn't say I've had any drastic changes. \n\nI do try to keep myself presentable, of course. I wouldn't want to let myself go. I suppose you might say that. It's just... well, loneliness is such a strong word, isn't it? It makes it sound like I'm just sitting here feeling sorry for myself, and I really try not to do that. \n\nI do miss her terribly, of course. We used to have our tea together on Tuesdays, and... well, when Tuesday rolls around now, it just feels a little empty. So I might make a little something just to... to fill the time, I suppose. Just to have something warm in the house. \n\nBut I wouldn't want you to think I'm completely falling apart or anything like that. I still go to church, and I see the other ladies. It's just a little comfort, really. Do you think it's normal to do that sometimes, when you miss someone? I hope I'm not sounding too foolish, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating these comfort foods is something you do more out of habit or because it helps you feel better when you're missing your friend?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... that's a very thoughtful question, isn't it? I... I'm not entirely sure, to be perfectly honest with you. \n\nI suppose it might just be a bit of a habit now? You know how it is when you get to be my age, you just get used to doing certain things at certain times. Like baking on a Tuesday afternoon. It's just what I've always done. \n\nI wouldn't say it's... well, I don't think it's necessarily because I'm sitting there feeling terribly sad about my friend, not all the time anyway. I mean, it does feel nice to have the house smell like cinnamon and sugar, it makes things feel a bit more cheerful. But I don't want to make it sound like I'm relying on it, or that I can't handle my feelings properly. I'm quite a strong person, usually. \n\nI just... I suppose I haven't really thought about whether it's a habit or a comfort thing. Is it important to know the difference, Doctor? I'll try to pay more attention if you think that would be helpful for us.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat these comfort foods in secret, hiding it from others?\n\nPatient: Oh... well, um... in secret? That sounds so... so deceitful, doesn't it? I really wouldn't want you to think I'm the sort of person who sneaks around or hides things from people. I've always tried to be very open and honest, I really have.\n\nI mean, I suppose... well, since I live alone, there isn't really anyone here to see what I'm eating anyway, is there? So it's not like I'm actively hiding it from anyone. If the ladies from church came by while I was having a slice of cake, I certainly wouldn't shove it in a drawer or anything silly like that! \n\nIt's just that... well, I usually have these little snacks when I'm on my own, just because that's when I happen to be home. But I wouldn't call it a secret, exactly. I hope that makes sense? I just don't want you to get the wrong idea about me, Doctor. I'm doing my best to be a good patient for you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, my weight? Well... I suppose I try not to fuss too much over the scale these days. At my age, you know, things just sort of shift around a bit naturally, don't they? \n\nI haven't noticed anything... well, nothing significant, I don't think. My clothes still fit well enough, mostly. Maybe a little snug around the middle now and then, but nothing to cause any alarm. I certainly wouldn't say I've had any drastic changes. \n\nI do try to keep myself presentable, of course. I wouldn't want to let myself go. Do you think a little fluctuation is alright for someone my age, Doctor? I hope I'm taking care of myself the way I ought to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you gained a significant amount of weight since this eating behavior started?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... well, no, I really don't believe so. Since I started baking a bit more, I mean. \n\nLike I mentioned just a moment ago, it's really just been a little fluctuation here and there. I haven't had to go out and buy a whole new wardrobe or anything dreadful like that! I've always been very mindful not to let my figure get completely away from me. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh... my friend? Oh, dear. Well... I suppose it's possible there's a little connection. I wouldn't want you to think I'm falling to pieces or anything dramatic like that. I've always tried to keep a stiff upper lip, as they say. 

But... well, yes, I suppose this all did start after she passed away. It's been about three months now. I just... I miss our afternoons together, that's all. We used to have tea, and now the house is just a bit quiet. So perhaps I have been having a few more treats since then, just to pass the time. But it's nothing to be overly concerned about, I promise. It's just a little adjustment period, isn't it? 

I hope it's alright that I didn't mention her right away, Doctor. I didn't want to seem like a burden, going on about my personal sorrows. Is it helpful for you to know that? I do want to make sure I'm doing this right.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Oh, hello, Doctor. Thank you so much for seeing me today. I hope I'm not taking up too much of your time. \n\nWell, I suppose I'm here because... oh, it's probably just a silly habit of an old woman, really. It's like I just can't make myself stop, even when I know I should be done. \n\nIs that... does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it right for you.\n\nDoctor: When you say your hand just keeps going back to the pot, do you feel like you're in control of your actions at that moment?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... well, it's a bit hard to admit. I suppose... no, I don't really feel like I'm completely in charge right then. \n\nI mean, I know it's me doing it, of course. But just like I was saying, once I start on those comfort foods, I'll be sitting there telling myself, \"Maria, that is enough now.\" But it's almost like my hand has a mind of its own, going back to the pot, and I just can't seem to make myself stop. \n\nIt sounds dreadful when I say it out loud, doesn't it? A woman my age ought to have more willpower. I hope I'm giving you the right kind of answers, Doctor, I really want to be helpful.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen only with comfort foods or can it also happen with other types of food?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness... you know, I haven't really thought to pay attention to that. I suppose sometimes I might be a little flustered or tired when I make them. Sometimes it's just... just a quiet evening, and I think a little taste would be nice. I try very hard not to let my moods get the better of me, Doctor, I really do. I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after eating these comfort foods?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... well... yes, I suppose I do. It's... it's terribly embarrassing to admit, Doctor. \n\nI try to tell myself it's just a bit of food, but... afterward, I just feel so ashamed. And sad, really. These are recipes I used to make for my husband, for the children when they were little. They used to mean family and caring for people. And now... now it feels like I've turned it into this... this secret thing that I have to hide from the ladies at church. \n\nI don't want anyone to know I behave that way. It's not very dignified for a woman my age, is it? I hope you don't think too poorly of me for it, Doctor. I really am trying to be honest with you so you can help me.\n\nDoctor: How often would you say this happens with your comfort foods?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... let me think. I suppose... well, it hasn't always been quite like this. It really started happening a bit more since my dear friend passed away recently. It's usually on those long, quiet afternoons, like after I get home from choir practice, or when I've finished up my gardening and there's just no one around to talk to. \n\nI really do try to keep myself busy and be good the rest of the time, Doctor, I promise. Just once or twice a week isn't too terribly bad, is it? I hope that's the sort of detail you needed... I just want to make sure I'm answering you properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating these comfort foods is a way for you to cope with feelings of loneliness since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Oh... well, I... It makes it sound like I'm just sitting here feeling sorry for myself, and I really try not to do that. \n\nI do miss her terribly, of course. We used to have our tea together on Tuesdays, and... well, when Tuesday rolls around now, it just feels a little empty. So I might make a little something just to... to fill the time, I suppose. Just to have something warm in the house. \n\nBut I wouldn't want you to think I'm completely falling apart or anything like that. I still go to church, and I see the other ladies. It's just a little comfort, really. Do you think it's normal to do that sometimes, when you miss someone? I hope I'm not sounding too foolish, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating these comfort foods is something you do more out of habit or because it helps you feel better when you're missing your friend?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... that's a very thoughtful question, isn't it? I... I'm not entirely sure, to be perfectly honest with you. \n\nI suppose it might just be a bit of a habit now? You know how it is when you get to be my age, you just get used to doing certain things at certain times. Like baking on a Tuesday afternoon. It's just what I've always done. \n\nI wouldn't say it's... well, I don't think it's necessarily because I'm sitting there feeling terribly sad about my friend, not all the time anyway. I mean, it does feel nice to have the house smell like cinnamon and sugar, it makes things feel a bit more cheerful. But I don't want to make it sound like I'm relying on it, or that I can't handle my feelings properly. I'm quite a strong person, usually. \n\nI just... I suppose I haven't really thought about whether it's a habit or a comfort thing. Is it important to know the difference, Doctor? I'll try to pay more attention if you think that would be helpful for us.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat these comfort foods in secret, hiding it from others?\n\nPatient: Oh... well, um... in secret? I've always tried to be very open and honest, I really have.\n\nI mean, I suppose... well, since I live alone, there isn't really anyone here to see what I'm eating anyway, is there? If the ladies from church came by while I was having a slice of cake, I certainly wouldn't shove it in a drawer or anything silly like that! \n\nIt's just that... well, I usually have these little snacks when I'm on my own, just because that's when I happen to be home. But I wouldn't call it a secret, exactly. I hope that makes sense? I just don't want you to get the wrong idea about me, Doctor. I'm doing my best to be a good patient for you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, my weight? Well... I suppose I try not to fuss too much over the scale these days. At my age, you know, things just sort of shift around a bit naturally, don't they? \n\nI haven't noticed anything... well, nothing significant, I don't think. My clothes still fit well enough, mostly. Maybe a little snug around the middle now and then, but nothing to cause any alarm. I certainly wouldn't say I've had any drastic changes. \n\nI do try to keep myself presentable, of course. I wouldn't want to let myself go. Do you think a little fluctuation is alright for someone my age, Doctor? I hope I'm taking care of myself the way I ought to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you gained a significant amount of weight since this eating behavior started?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... well, no, I really don't believe so. Since I started baking a bit more, I mean. \n\nLike I mentioned just a moment ago, it's really just been a little fluctuation here and there. I haven't had to go out and buy a whole new wardrobe or anything dreadful like that! I've always been very mindful not to let my figure get completely away from me. I... I'm not entirely sure, to be perfectly honest with you. \n\nI suppose it might just be a bit of a habit now? You know how it is when you get to be my age, you just get used to doing certain things at certain times. Like baking on a Tuesday afternoon. It's just what I've always done. \n\nI wouldn't say it's... well, I don't think it's necessarily because I'm sitting there feeling terribly sad about my friend, not all the time anyway. I mean, it does feel nice to have the house smell like cinnamon and sugar, it makes things feel a bit more cheerful. But I don't want to make it sound like I'm relying on it, or that I can't handle my feelings properly. I'm quite a strong person, usually. \n\nI just... I suppose I haven't really thought about whether it's a habit or a comfort thing. Is it important to know the difference, Doctor? I'll try to pay more attention if you think that would be helpful for us.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat these comfort foods in secret, hiding it from others?\n\nPatient: Oh... well, um... in secret? That sounds so... so deceitful, doesn't it? I really wouldn't want you to think I'm the sort of person who sneaks around or hides things from people. I've always tried to be very open and honest, I really have.\n\nI mean, I suppose... well, since I live alone, there isn't really anyone here to see what I'm eating anyway, is there? So it's not like I'm actively hiding it from anyone. If the ladies from church came by while I was having a slice of cake, I certainly wouldn't shove it in a drawer or anything silly like that! \n\nIt's just that... well, I usually have these little snacks when I'm on my own, just because that's when I happen to be home. But I wouldn't call it a secret, exactly. I hope that makes sense? I just don't want you to get the wrong idea about me, Doctor. I'm doing my best to be a good patient for you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, my weight? Well... I suppose I try not to fuss too much over the scale these days. At my age, you know, things just sort of shift around a bit naturally, don't they? \n\nI haven't noticed anything... well, nothing significant, I don't think. My clothes still fit well enough, mostly. Maybe a little snug around the middle now and then, but nothing to cause any alarm. I certainly wouldn't say I've had any drastic changes. \n\nI do try to keep myself presentable, of course. I wouldn't want to let myself go. Do you think a little fluctuation is alright for someone my age, Doctor? I hope I'm taking care of myself the way I ought to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you gained a significant amount of weight since this eating behavior started?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... well, no, I really don't believe so. I haven't had to go out and buy a whole new wardrobe or anything dreadful like that! I've always been very mindful not to let my figure get completely away from me. I hope you don't think I look like I've gained a terrible amount, Doctor? I really am trying my best to stay healthy and do the right things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed with emotions when you catch yourself eating these comfort foods uncontrollably?\n\nPatient: Oh... well... overwhelmed? I suppose... well, afterward, I do feel a bit... down. Just a little sad, really. And maybe a tiny bit ashamed. It's just that... well, baking and having a nice treat, it always used to be about family, didn't it? About showing you care. And now... well, now it feels like something I wouldn't want the people at church to know about. \n\nIt makes me feel a bit foolish, to be honest. Turning something so lovely into something I feel I ought to keep quiet. I hope you don't think too badly of me for feeling that way, Doctor? I know it's silly to get upset over a bit of cake, and I really am trying to be sensible about it all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating these comfort foods is a way to escape or avoid feeling certain emotions?\n\nPatient: Oh, escape? Goodness, no, I wouldn't say that at all. That sounds so... so troubled, doesn't it? Like someone who can't handle their own life. I've always been very capable of managing things, even when times get a bit tough. \n\nI really don't think I'm running away from anything. It's just... well, sometimes a nice piece of pie or some fresh cookies just make the house feel a little warmer, a little less quiet. It's more of a comfort, really, just something familiar. It's not about avoiding anything, just... enjoying a small pleasure. \n\nI'm quite sure I don't use it to hide from my feelings. That wouldn't be very healthy, would it? I always try to face things properly. Oh, in the company of others? Goodness, no. I wouldn't want to make a fuss or... or seem greedy in front of anyone else. 

I mean, if I had guests over, I'd certainly offer them a biscuit with their tea, but I wouldn't sit there eating a whole plate myself. That wouldn't be very polite, would it? I've always been taught to be a gracious hostess, to make sure everyone else has what they need first. 

I suppose... well, I suppose I only really have these little treats when I'm on my own. It's just a quiet thing I do by myself. I wouldn't want anyone to see me making a mess or... or eating more than my fair share. I'd hate for anyone to think poorly of me for it.

I mean, it does feel nice to have the house smell like cinnamon and sugar, it makes things feel a bit more cheerful. But I don't want to make it sound like I'm relying on it, or that I can't handle my feelings properly. I'm quite a strong person, usually. \n\nI just... I suppose I haven't really thought about whether it's a habit or a comfort thing. Is it important to know the difference, Doctor? I'll try to pay more attention if you think that would be helpful for us.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat these comfort foods in secret, hiding it from others?\n\nPatient: Oh... well, um... in secret? That sounds so... so deceitful, doesn't it? I really wouldn't want you to think I'm the sort of person who sneaks around or hides things from people. I've always tried to be very open and honest, I really have.\n\nI mean, I suppose... well, since I live alone, there isn't really anyone here to see what I'm eating anyway, is there? So it's not like I'm actively hiding it from anyone. If the ladies from church came by while I was having a slice of cake, I certainly wouldn't shove it in a drawer or anything silly like that! \n\nIt's just that... well, I usually have these little snacks when I'm on my own, just because that's when I happen to be home. But I wouldn't call it a secret, exactly. I hope that makes sense? I just don't want you to get the wrong idea about me, Doctor. I'm doing my best to be a good patient for you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, my weight? Well... I suppose I try not to fuss too much over the scale these days. At my age, you know, things just sort of shift around a bit naturally, don't they? \n\nI haven't noticed anything... well, nothing significant, I don't think. My clothes still fit well enough, mostly. Maybe a little snug around the middle now and then, but nothing to cause any alarm. I certainly wouldn't say I've had any drastic changes. \n\nI do try to keep myself presentable, of course. I wouldn't want to let myself go. Do you think a little fluctuation is alright for someone my age, Doctor? I hope I'm taking care of myself the way I ought to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you gained a significant amount of weight since this eating behavior started?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... well, no, I really don't believe so. Since I started baking a bit more, I mean. \n\nLike I mentioned just a moment ago, it's really just been a little fluctuation here and there. I haven't had to go out and buy a whole new wardrobe or anything dreadful like that! I've always been very mindful not to let my figure get completely away from me. It just wouldn't be proper, would it? \n\nSo, no, nothing significant at all. I haven't kept a strict eye on the scale, but I'm quite sure I haven't put on a large amount. I hope you don't think I look like I've gained a terrible amount, Doctor? I really am trying my best to stay healthy and do the right things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed with emotions when you catch yourself eating these comfort foods uncontrollably?\n\nPatient: Oh... well... overwhelmed? I suppose... well, afterward, I do feel a bit... down. Just a little sad, really. And maybe a tiny bit ashamed. It's just that... well, baking and having a nice treat, it always used to be about family, didn't it? About showing you care. And now... well, now it feels like something I wouldn't want the people at church to know about. \n\nIt makes me feel a bit foolish, to be honest. Turning something so lovely into something I feel I ought to keep quiet. I hope you don't think too badly of me for feeling that way, Doctor? I know it's silly to get upset over a bit of cake, and I really am trying to be sensible about it all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating these comfort foods is a way to escape or avoid feeling certain emotions?\n\nPatient: Oh, escape? Goodness, no, I wouldn't say that at all. That sounds so... so troubled, doesn't it? Like someone who can't handle their own life. I've always been very capable of managing things, even when times get a bit tough. \n\nI really don't think I'm running away from anything. It's just... well, sometimes a nice piece of pie or some fresh cookies just make the house feel a little warmer, a little less quiet. It's more of a comfort, really, just something familiar. It's not about avoiding anything, just... enjoying a small pleasure. \n\nI'm quite sure I don't use it to hide from my feelings. That wouldn't be very healthy, would it? I always try to face things properly. I... well, I really haven't noticed anything like that. I don't think I sit down with a slice of cake just because I'm feeling a certain way. That sounds a bit... well, a bit unstable, doesn't it? And I've always prided myself on having a fairly steady disposition. \n\nI just... I suppose I just think they taste nice, and it's a pleasant way to pass a quiet afternoon. Another activity? Oh, goodness... I suppose I haven't really thought about it quite like that. I'm not entirely sure, to be perfectly honest with you. 

I do try to keep my hands busy with my cross-stitch and tidying up the sitting room, but... well, if you think finding a new hobby or a little club would be the right thing to do, I'd certainly be very happy to try. I wouldn't want to just sit about being foolish with my time if there's a better way to go about my day. I'd like to think I could easily give up those little treats if I had something else to focus on... at least, I hope I could. 

I'd certainly make my best effort, anyway. Does that sound like a sensible plan to you, Doctor? I really do want to do whatever you think is best, I just wouldn't want to promise anything and then disappoint you. Do you think a new activity is what I ought to be looking into?

I suppose... no, I don't really feel like I'm completely in charge right then. \n\nI mean, I know it's me doing it, of course. But just like I was saying, once I start on those comfort foods, I'll be sitting there telling myself, \"Maria, that is enough now.\" But it's almost like my hand has a mind of its own, going back to the pot, and I just can't seem to make myself stop. \n\nIt sounds dreadful when I say it out loud, doesn't it? A woman my age ought to have more willpower. I hope I'm giving you the right kind of answers, Doctor, I really want to be helpful.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen only with comfort foods or can it also happen with other types of food?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness... you know, I haven't really thought to pay attention to that. I suppose... well, it really is mostly just my special recipes. The things I've cooked for years, you know, my little comforts. \n\nI don't think I'd just sit there and do that with... oh, I don't know, just anything out of the cupboard. At least, I haven't noticed myself doing it with other things. I really do try to be very good most of the time, I promise! It's just those certain pots I make. \n\nIs that... is that the sort of answer you were looking for, Doctor? I want to make sure I'm giving you all the right details.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat these comfort foods when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I suppose sometimes I might be a little flustered or tired when I make them. You know how it is, a warm bowl of something familiar is just so soothing at the end of a long day. \n\nBut I wouldn't say I only eat them when I'm upset, no. Sometimes it's just... just a quiet evening, and I think a little taste would be nice. I try very hard not to let my moods get the better of me, Doctor, I really do. I don't want you to think I'm just sitting around crying and eating all day! It's just a little bit of comfort now and then. \n\nDoes that make sense? I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after eating these comfort foods?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... well... yes, I suppose I do. It's... it's terribly embarrassing to admit, Doctor. \n\nI try to tell myself it's just a bit of food, but... afterward, I just feel so ashamed. And sad, really. These are recipes I used to make for my husband, for the children when they were little. They used to mean family and caring for people. And now... now it feels like I've turned it into this... this secret thing that I have to hide from the ladies at church. \n\nI don't want anyone to know I behave that way. It's not very dignified for a woman my age, is it? I hope you don't think too poorly of me for it, Doctor. I really am trying to be honest with you so you can help me.\n\nDoctor: How often would you say this happens with your comfort foods?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... let me think. I suppose... well, it hasn't always been quite like this. It really started happening a bit more since my dear friend passed away recently. The house just gets so very quiet, you know? \n\nI'd say it happens most weeks now. But certainly not every day, oh goodness no! I make sure of that. Just... maybe once or twice a week. It's usually on those long, quiet afternoons, like after I get home from choir practice, or when I've finished up my gardening and there's just no one around to talk to. \n\nI really do try to keep myself busy and be good the rest of the time, Doctor, I promise. Just once or twice a week isn't too terribly bad, is it? I hope that's the sort of detail you needed... I just want to make sure I'm answering you properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating these comfort foods is a way for you to cope with feelings of loneliness since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Oh... well, I... I suppose you might say that. It's just... well, loneliness is such a strong word, isn't it? It makes it sound like I'm just sitting here feeling sorry for myself, and I really try not to do that. \n\nI do miss her terribly, of course. We used to have our tea together on Tuesdays, and... well, when Tuesday rolls around now, it just feels a little empty. So I might make a little something just to... to fill the time, I suppose. Just to have something warm in the house. \n\nBut I wouldn't want you to think I'm completely falling apart or anything like that. I still go to church, and I see the other ladies. It's just a little comfort, really. Do you think it's normal to do that sometimes, when you miss someone? I hope I'm not sounding too foolish, Doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating these comfort foods is something you do more out of habit or because it helps you feel better when you're missing your friend?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... that's a very thoughtful question, isn't it? I... I'm not entirely sure, to be perfectly honest with you. \n\nI suppose it might just be a bit of a habit now? You know how it is when you get to be my age, you just get used to doing certain things at certain times. Like baking on a Tuesday afternoon. It's just what I've always done. \n\nI wouldn't say it's... well, I don't think it's necessarily because I'm sitting there feeling terribly sad about my friend, not all the time anyway. I mean, it does feel nice to have the house smell like cinnamon and sugar, it makes things feel a bit more cheerful. But I don't want to make it sound like I'm relying on it, or that I can't handle my feelings properly. I'm quite a strong person, usually. \n\nI just... I suppose I haven't really thought about whether it's a habit or a comfort thing. Is it important to know the difference, Doctor? I'll try to pay more attention if you think that would be helpful for us.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat these comfort foods in secret, hiding it from others?\n\nPatient: Oh... well, um... in secret? That sounds so... so deceitful, doesn't it? I really wouldn't want you to think I'm the sort of person who sneaks around or hides things from people. I've always tried to be very open and honest, I really have.\n\nI mean, I suppose... well, since I live alone, there isn't really anyone here to see what I'm eating anyway, is there? So it's not like I'm actively hiding it from anyone. If the ladies from church came by while I was having a slice of cake, I certainly wouldn't shove it in a drawer or anything silly like that! \n\nIt's just that... well, I usually have these little snacks when I'm on my own, just because that's when I happen to be home. But I wouldn't call it a secret, exactly. I hope that makes sense? I just don't want you to get the wrong idea about me, Doctor. I'm doing my best to be a good patient for you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, my weight? Well... I suppose I try not to fuss too much over the scale these days. At my age, you know, things just sort of shift around a bit naturally, don't they? \n\nI haven't noticed anything... well, nothing significant, I don't think. My clothes still fit well enough, mostly. Maybe a little snug around the middle now and then, but nothing to cause any alarm. I certainly wouldn't say I've had any drastic changes. \n\nI do try to keep myself presentable, of course. I wouldn't want to let myself go. Do you think a little fluctuation is alright for someone my age, Doctor? I hope I'm taking care of myself the way I ought to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you gained a significant amount of weight since this eating behavior started?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear... well, no, I really don't believe so. Since I started baking a bit more, I mean. \n\nLike I mentioned just a moment ago, it's really just been a little fluctuation here and there. I haven't had to go out and buy a whole new wardrobe or anything dreadful like that! I've always been very mindful not to let my figure get completely away from me. It just wouldn't be proper, would it? \n\nSo, no, nothing significant at all. I haven't kept a strict eye on the scale, but I'm quite sure I haven't put on a large amount. I hope you don't think I look like I've gained a terrible amount, Doctor? I really am trying my best to stay healthy and do the right things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed with emotions when you catch yourself eating these comfort foods uncontrollably?\n\nPatient: Oh... well... overwhelmed? It's just that... well, baking and having a nice treat, it always used to be about family, didn't it? About showing you care. Turning something so lovely into something I feel I ought to keep quiet. I hope you don't think too badly of me for feeling that way, Doctor? I know it's silly to get upset over a bit of cake, and I really am trying to be sensible about it all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating these comfort foods is a way to escape or avoid feeling certain emotions?\n\nPatient: Oh, escape? Goodness, no, I wouldn't say that at all. That sounds so... so troubled, doesn't it? Like someone who can't handle their own life. I've always been very capable of managing things, even when times get a bit tough. \n\nI really don't think I'm running away from anything. It's just... well, sometimes a nice piece of pie or some fresh cookies just make the house feel a little warmer, a little less quiet. It's more of a comfort, really, just something familiar. It's not about avoiding anything, just... enjoying a small pleasure. \n\nI'm quite sure I don't use it to hide from my feelings. That wouldn't be very healthy, would it? I always try to face things properly. I... well, I really haven't noticed anything like that. I don't think I sit down with a slice of cake just because I'm feeling a certain way. That sounds a bit... well, a bit unstable, doesn't it? And I've always prided myself on having a fairly steady disposition. \n\nI just... I suppose I just think they taste nice, and it's a pleasant way to pass a quiet afternoon. I don't believe there's any deep, complicated reason behind it. I mean, everyone enjoys a little treat, don't they? Like some sort of mindless habit. \n\nI really haven't noticed it being anything like that. I mean, sometimes I might just be puttering around the kitchen and decide a little something would be nice, but I wouldn't say I do it just out of routine when I'm feeling absolutely nothing. I always try to be quite sensible about how I spend my days, you see. I wouldn't want you to think I'm just sitting there eating out of sheer boredom or habit. That wouldn't be very proper at all.\n\nDoes that answer what you were asking, Doctor? I do hope I'm explaining myself properly for you. I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating these comfort foods helps you feel better emotionally?\n\nPatient: Oh, feel better emotionally? Well... It's just a simple joy, not a... a coping mechanism or whatever they call it on the television. \n\nI hope that makes sense? When I'm feeling cheerful—like if my neighbor pops round for a chat or I've had a nice phone call with my niece—I don't really feel the need to... to have those little extras. I'm just enjoying the moment, you see. It's really just when the house gets so awfully quiet. 

So, no, I don't believe I'd be sitting there eating like that if I were perfectly content. I hope that's the right answer... I mean, I hope that makes sense to you, Doctor. Is it quite normal to only want those things when you're feeling a bit low? I do hope I'm explaining myself properly.

I really am trying my best to stay healthy and do the right things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed with emotions when you catch yourself eating these comfort foods uncontrollably?\n\nPatient: Oh... well... overwhelmed? That's a very strong word, isn't it? I wouldn't want you to think I'm sitting around being hysterical or anything like that. I've always prided myself on keeping a level head.\n\nBut... I suppose... well, afterward, I do feel a bit... down. Just a little sad, really. And maybe a tiny bit ashamed. It's just that... well, baking and having a nice treat, it always used to be about family, didn't it? About showing you care. And now... well, now it feels like something I wouldn't want the people at church to know about. \n\nIt makes me feel a bit foolish, to be honest. Turning something so lovely into something I feel I ought to keep quiet. I hope you don't think too badly of me for feeling that way, Doctor? I know it's silly to get upset over a bit of cake, and I really am trying to be sensible about it all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating these comfort foods is a way to escape or avoid feeling certain emotions?\n\nPatient: Oh, escape? Goodness, no, I wouldn't say that at all. That sounds so... so troubled, doesn't it? Like someone who can't handle their own life. I've always been very capable of managing things, even when times get a bit tough. \n\nI really don't think I'm running away from anything. It's just... well, sometimes a nice piece of pie or some fresh cookies just make the house feel a little warmer, a little less quiet. It's more of a comfort, really, just something familiar. It's not about avoiding anything, just... enjoying a small pleasure. \n\nI'm quite sure I don't use it to hide from my feelings. That wouldn't be very healthy, would it? I always try to face things properly. You don't think I'm doing something terribly wrong just by wanting a little comfort now and then, do you, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Do you think the feeling of comfort you get from eating these foods is related to the emotions you're experiencing at the time?\n\nPatient: Oh... goodness, related to my emotions? I... well, I really haven't noticed anything like that. I don't think I sit down with a slice of cake just because I'm feeling a certain way. That sounds a bit... well, a bit unstable, doesn't it? And I've always prided myself on having a fairly steady disposition. \n\nI just... I suppose I just think they taste nice, and it's a pleasant way to pass a quiet afternoon. I don't believe there's any deep, complicated reason behind it. I mean, everyone enjoys a little treat, don't they? It's perfectly normal to want something sweet with a cup of tea. \n\nI hope you don't think I'm being difficult or hiding anything from you, Doctor. I'm just trying to answer as honestly as I can. Am I giving you the right sort of information? I do want to be helpful.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you eat these comfort foods when you're not feeling any particular emotion, just out of routine?\n\nPatient: Oh... a routine? Well, I'm not sure I'd say that, exactly. That makes it sound a bit like... like I'm just eating like clockwork, doesn't it? Like some sort of mindless habit. \n\nI really haven't noticed it being anything like that. I mean, sometimes I might just be puttering around the kitchen and decide a little something would be nice, but I wouldn't say I do it just out of routine when I'm feeling absolutely nothing. I always try to be quite sensible about how I spend my days, you see. I wouldn't want you to think I'm just sitting there eating out of sheer boredom or habit. That wouldn't be very proper at all.\n\nDoes that answer what you were asking, Doctor? I do hope I'm explaining myself properly for you. I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating these comfort foods helps you feel better emotionally?\n\nPatient: Oh, feel better emotionally? Well... I suppose that makes it sound like I'm using food as some kind of medicine, doesn't it? And I really don't think I'm doing that. \n\nI mean, certainly, a warm cookie is pleasant, and it brings a little smile to my face, but... I wouldn't say it's fixing any deep sadness or anything like that. I've always been a rather cheerful person, really. I just think nice food tastes nice, that's all. It's just a simple joy, not a... a coping mechanism or whatever they call it on the television. \n\nI hope that makes sense? I just wouldn't want you to worry that I'm relying on sweets to manage my feelings. That wouldn't be very sensible of me, would it? Am I making the right sort of sense, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Do you think the loss of your friend has anything to do with your recent eating habits?\n\nPatient: Oh... my friend? Oh, dear. Well... I suppose it's possible there's a little connection. I wouldn't want you to think I'm falling to pieces or anything dramatic like that. I've always tried to keep a stiff upper lip, as they say. \n\nBut... well, yes, I suppose this all did start after she passed away. It's been about three months now. I just... I miss our afternoons together, that's all. We used to have tea, and now the house is just a bit quiet. So perhaps I have been having a few more treats since then, just to pass the time. But it's nothing to be overly concerned about, I promise. It's just a little adjustment period, isn't it? \n\nI hope it's alright that I didn't mention her right away, Doctor. I didn't want to seem like a burden, going on about my personal sorrows. Is it helpful for you to know that? I do want to make sure I'm doing this right.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat these comfort foods as frequently if you were in the company of others?\n\nPatient: Oh, in the company of others? Goodness, no. I wouldn't want to make a fuss or... or seem greedy in front of anyone else. \n\nI mean, if I had guests over, I'd certainly offer them a biscuit with their tea, but I wouldn't sit there eating a whole plate myself. That wouldn't be very polite, would it? I've always been taught to be a gracious hostess, to make sure everyone else has what they need first. \n\nI suppose... well, I suppose I only really have these little treats when I'm on my own. It's just a quiet thing I do by myself. I wouldn't want anyone to see me making a mess or... or eating more than my fair share. I'd hate for anyone to think poorly of me for it. \n\nDoes that make sense, Doctor? I do hope I'm not sounding too silly about it all. I just try to keep up appearances, you know? Is that the sort of thing you were wondering about?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to stop eating these comfort foods if you found another activity to fill your quiet afternoons?\n\nPatient: Another activity? Oh, goodness... I suppose I haven't really thought about it quite like that. I'm not entirely sure, to be perfectly honest with you. \n\nI do try to keep my hands busy with my cross-stitch and tidying up the sitting room, but... well, if you think finding a new hobby or a little club would be the right thing to do, I'd certainly be very happy to try. I wouldn't want to just sit about being foolish with my time if there's a better way to go about my day. I'd like to think I could easily give up those little treats if I had something else to focus on... at least, I hope I could. \n\nI'd certainly make my best effort, anyway. Does that sound like a sensible plan to you, Doctor? I really do want to do whatever you think is best, I just wouldn't want to promise anything and then disappoint you. Do you think a new activity is what I ought to be looking into?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat these comfort foods when you're feeling happy or content?\n\nPatient: Oh, when I'm happy? Well... I suppose I haven't really paid close attention to that. I don't think so? \n\nWhen I'm feeling cheerful—like if my neighbor pops round for a chat or I've had a nice phone call with my niece—I don't really feel the need to... to have those little extras. I'm just enjoying the moment, you see. It's really just when the house gets so awfully quiet. \n\nSo, no, I don't believe I'd be sitting there eating like that if I were perfectly content. I hope that's the right answer... I mean, I hope that makes sense to you, Doctor. Is it quite normal to only want those things when you're feeling a bit low? I do hope I'm explaining myself properly."
